公司研究報告 
本報告僅供宏遠投顧內部及客戶參考，雖已力求正確與完整，但因時間及市場客觀因素改變所造成產業、市場
或個股之相關條件改變，投資人需自行考量投資之實際狀況與風險，本公司恕不負擔任何法律責任及做任何保
證。非經本公司同意，不得將本報告加以引用或轉載

2368金像電 
(Gold Circuit Electronics)

報 告 日 期 ：2026/09/22 研 究 員： 方姿婷  (Lesley  Fang ) 
壹、投 資 建 議 
重點摘要：  
⚫ 2Q26 營收 符 合 預 期，毛 利 率 受人 民 幣 升 值 匯 率 干 擾、直 接 人 工
成 本 增 加 影 響，毛 利 率 略 低 於35%，展 望 3Q26 毛 利 率 應 可 回 歸
常態增至 37~38% 之間 。 
⚫ AI 伺 服 器 帶 動 高 階 銅 箔 基 板與 PCB(HDI 、HLC) 厚板 需 求 強 勁，
2H26 ASIC 客戶 (AWS Trainium 4 及 Google TPU V9) 等，預計採
用 HDI 與 HLC 混 壓 設 計 ， 金 像 電 於3~4 階 HDI 混壓技術已經
相 對 純 熟 可 順 利 供 應 。 
⚫ 2H26 至 2027 年， CCL /PCB 產 業 升 級 朝 向1.6T Switch 發 展 ， 預
期 板 層 數 提 升 至44~52L 、CCL 材 料 端 為 M8 或 M9 等 級 供 應 ，
對 於 金 像 電 而 言 ，PCB 板 層 數 提 升 幅 度 達15~20% 。

一. 2Q26 營收與財報 符合市場預期， 3Q26 展望正向  
金像電 2Q26 營收 242.79 億元， +25.71%QoQ ，+75.26%YoY ， 毛 利
率 34.65% ，稅 後 淨 利 47.83 億 元，稅 後 EPS+9.24 元。對於 2H26 看
法，因 應 客 戶 強 勁 需 求(AI ASIC 厚板 )，產 能 擴 充 方 面，新 增 蘇 州、
泰國新廠 HDI 厚 板 支 應。展望 3Q26 ，累計 7~8 月 份 金 像 電 營 收 達
成率 67% 符 合 市 場 預 期 ， 預 估 金 像 電 營 收 為 305.86 億 元 ，
+25.98%QoQ ， 毛 利 率 37.55% ， 稅 後 淨 利 67.04 億 元 ， 單 季 稅 後
EPS+12.72 元。

二. AI 伺服器帶動 CCL/PCB 產值提升，產能擴充 積極  
宏遠投顧觀察 ，AI 伺 服 器 與 先 進 封 裝 等 需 求 日 增 ，同 時 對 於 一 般
型 伺 服 器 用 板、高 階Switch 用 板、AI 伺 服 器 用 板，需 求 迅 速 拉 升。
回顧近年來 ，金 像 電 產 能 擴 充，各 廠 區 台 灣 廠30 億 元、蘇 州 廠 38
億 元、常 熟 一 廠 10 億 元、常 熟 二 廠 3 億 元，2H26 ASIC 客戶 (AWS 
Trainium 4 及 Google TPU V9) 等，預 計 採 用 HDI 與 HLC 混 壓 設 計，
金像電於 3~4 階 HDI 混 壓 技 術 已 經 相 對 純 熟 ， 對 於2H26~2027 年
未 來 訂 單 能 見 度 影 響 不 大， 未 來 出 貨 將 可 順 利 供 貨。2027 年隨著
NVIDIA  Rubin 平 台 將 帶 動CCL M8+ 、M9 材 料 出 貨 攀 升 ，AI ASIC 
CCL 拉 貨 動 能呈現 50~60% 高 度 成 長 ，CCL 、PCB 產 品 組 合 持 續 優
化。2027 年 起，CCL 與 PCB 產 業 產 品 升 級，除一般型伺服器 Intel 
Diamond Rapids 平 台 、AMD Zen6 Venice 平 台 採 用PCIe Gen6 與 Ultra 
Low Loss CCL(M7) ，PCB 板層數增至 20~24L ；2027 年 ， 隨 著1.6T 
投資建議：買進 
目標價位：1,290.00 元 
      16.00X 2027 EPS

參考價位：1,045.00 元 
     12.97X 2027 EPS 
     2026/09/21 收盤

或個股之相關條件改變，投資人需自行考量投資之實際狀況與風險，本公司恕不負擔任何法律責任及做任何保
證。非經本公司同意，不得將本報告加以引用或轉載

2 
Switch 逐步導入 M8/M9 CCL(HLC 層數 46~52L 、 玻 纖 Low Dk2/Q
布、銅箔 HVLP4/5) ， 預 期 CCL 廠 商 產 品 規 格 顯 著 升 級 。

三. 金像 電對標高階 Switch 板材， 2027 年為成長主軸  
金像電 於 AI 伺 服 器 與新 平 台之 間 設 計 資 料 傳 輸 速 率、資 料 交 換 與
處 理 等 級 提 升 ， 對 於 高 階Switch 網 路 交 換 器 需 求 同 步 增 加 。觀察
AI 產 業 發 展，高 階Switch 於 2026 年 已 全 面 升 級 至800GSwitch ，金
像電 過往 為 品 牌(企業用 Switch )與 白 牌 主 要 交 換 器PCB 供 應 商 ，
以 800G Switch 為 例，PCB 板層數 38~44L 設 計、CCL 材料端為 M8
等 級，金 像 電 營 收 網 通 產 品 已 達10~20% 當 中 多 數 為 高 階Switch 板
材，展 望 2027 年 CCL/PCB 產 業 升 級 朝 向1.6T Switch 發 展，預 期 板
層數提升至 44~52L 、CCL 材 料 端 為 M8 或 M9 等級 供 應 ， 對 於 金
像電而言， PCB 板 層 數 提 升 幅 度 達15~20% 。

四.預估 2026/2027 年稅後 EPS 43.68 /80.57 元 
2026 年受惠 AI 伺 服 器及 ASIC 需 求 持 續 強 勁 的 帶 動 下 ，金像 電為
AI PCB 領 導 廠 商與 AI ASIC 厚 板 主 要 供 甕 商， 將 受 惠AI Server 對
於高 階 厚 板 與HLC 板 材、HDI 板材 強 勁 的 需 求。因而 ，預估 2026
年 金像 電 營 收 為1,086 .36 億元， +81.04%YoY ， 毛 利 率36.75%，稅
後淨利 228 .27 億 元，稅 後 EPS+ 43.68 元。展 望 2027 年，在 AI 伺服
器 需 求 持 續 強 勁 的 帶 動 下 ， 帶 動金像電 PCB 產能 持續 增長， 預估
2027 年 金像 電營收為 1,749 .83 億元， +61.07%YoY ，毛利率為
40.01%， 稅 後 淨 利424.59 億元，稅後 EPS+ 80.57 元。

四.投資建議維持 買進，目標價 1,290 元 
宏 遠 投 顧 認 為，金像 電 營 運 重 點 在 於，營 運 動 能來 自 於一 般 型 伺 服
器 CPU 廠商 Intel、AMD 均 有 新 品 帶 動 新 平 台 升 級 與 相 關 供 應 的 零
組 件 商 機，以 及 攻 入AI 伺 服 器、高 階 網 路 交 換 器與 一 般 型 伺 服 器
主板 規 格 轉 換 成 長 可 期。基 於 上 述，給 予金像 電 投 資 建 議 為 買 進，
目標價 1,290 元(16.00x 20 27 EPS) 。

貳、公 司 概 況

一. 公司簡介  
金像 電(23 68)為 PCB 廠 商 ，為 全 球 最 大 一 般 型 伺 服 器 PCB 製 造 商 ，
產品應用別 當 中，伺 服 器 與AI 伺 服 器 營 收 比 重 達78% 、網 通 產 品
(包 含 高 階Switch) 達 10~20% 、 其 他 為NB 佔比 5%、 與 其 他 品 項 。

二.2026~2027 年營運概況 及展望  
1.HDI 與 M8 以 上 升 級 仍 為 關 注 焦 點 ，2026~2028 年 產 能 建 置 積 極 
過 往 一 般 型 伺 服 器 用 板 採用 HDI 設計 ， 強 調 細 線 路 設 計 ，CPU 尺
寸變大 Pin C ount 數 量 增 加 至2,000 只~4,000 只 Pin Count ，PCB 板
面積不變之下要走 向 細 線 路化(線寬 /線距 )朝 向 更 細 線 路 設 計 。 製
程上 HDI 經 過 多 次 壓 合 後，PP 在 熱 壓 多 次後，每 一 層 熱 膨 脹 係 數
不同，導致通孔 Crack 或翹曲，製作時容易產生爆板，因此對於
PCB 板廠製作良率會是一大技術難關，而金像電、健鼎等廠商於
高階 HDI 相 對 具 備 生 產 優 勢 與 競 爭 力 ， 相 對 應 台 光 電 、 台 燿 等 高
階 CCL 供 貨 與 升 級 也 成 為 製 程 一 大 瓶 頸 ；回 顧 近 年 來 ， 金 像 電 產
能 擴 充 ， 各 廠 區 台 灣 廠30 億 元 、 蘇 州 廠38 億 元 、 常 熟 一 廠10 億

一. 2Q26 毛利率 略低於 市場預期 ，單季 EPS 為 9.24 元 
金像電 2Q26 營收 242.79 億元， +25.71%QoQ ，+75.26%YoY ， 毛 利
率 34.65% ， 稅 後 淨 利47.83 億 元 ， 稅 後EPS+9.24 元 。 展 望3Q26 ，
累計 7~8 月 份 金 像 電 營 收 達 成 率67% 符 合 市 場 預 期 ，預 估 金 像 電

二. AI 伺服器拉貨動能續強、高階網路交換器與 一般型
伺服器板材 進入量產， 估 2026 年 EPS+ 43.68 元、估 2027
年 EPS+ 80.57 元

Intel 、AMD 均 有 新 品 帶 動 新 平 台 升 級 與 相 關 供 應 的 零 組 件 商 機 ，
以及攻入 AI 伺 服 器、高 階 網 路 交 換 器 與 一 般 型 伺 服 器 主 板 規 格 轉
換成長可期 。 預估 2026 年 金 像 電 營 收 為 1,086.36 億元，

元。展 望 2027 年，在 AI 伺服器 需 求 持 續 強 勁 的 帶 動 下，帶 動金像

### Page 2

Coherent Corp (COHR.K)
21 September 2026 Citi Research
2
Strategy and positioning: Management launched PhotonLink as an architecture-
agnostic integrated optics platform covering CPO, NPO, and chip-to-chip optical
connections. It spans the full optical chain: Generating the light, shaping and
guiding it, and detecting it at the far end. Management discussed that its
customers asked it to supply complete assemblies rather than individual
components as stitching together lasers, fiber and passive optics is difficult. It

argued that no other company holds the full portfolio in-house and that Coherent
can manufacture at datacenter scale. Management expects a mix of adoption
paths, with some customers going straight to CPO and others starting with NPO
and moving to CPO later.
Figure 1. COHR's Portfolio of Optical AI Networking Products
Source: COHR at ECOC
Customers and content: In a full-solution example (combining the ELS, the
assembly to the switch or XPU, and the assembly out to the faceplate),

management said Coherent content could reach up to $15k of content per
switch/processor chip needing 100T of I/O capacity at 200G optical lane. Coherent
counts more than 10 customer engagements each in CPO and NPO and more than
5 in chip-to-chip, with activity going up sharply over the past six months. For CPO,
management said it has an anchor customer with an LTA secured, which it
identified as NVDA. It also has an anchor customer and LTA for NPO, which it did

not name. Separately, management cited two LTAs for ultra-high-power CW lasers
with leading hyperscale or AI datacenter customers, also unnamed.

### Page 3

Coherent Corp (COHR.K)
21 September 2026 Citi Research
3
Figure 2. PhontonLink Platform
Source: COHR at ECOC 2026
Timing: CPO for scale-out, tied to the NVIDIA agreement, begins ramping in the
December quarter (calendar Q4 2026). CPO for scale-up follows in the second half
of calendar 2027. NPO ramps in the second half of next calendar year, mainly for
scale-up. Chip-to-chip is further out, around 2029-2030.
Light generation: The ultra-high-power CW laser sits in an external laser source

that holds up to eight lasers, each feeding roughly four links. It starts ramping this
quarter, with significant volume next year, on the 6-inch InP platform in Sherman,
Texas. For NPO, a high-power buried-heterostructure CW laser is already shipping
in millions of units per month for pluggables. VCSEL arrays come in a conventional
version for ribbon fiber and a high-density 2D version that can be flip-chipped
directly to drivers and TIAs with backside lenses. Photodetectors run to 200G

today, with 400G in development.
Figure 3. UHP CW Laser Leader
Source: COHR at ECOC 2026

### Page 4

Coherent Corp (COHR.K)
21 September 2026 Citi Research
4
Fab footprint: Sherman runs 6-inch InP and GaAs, Sweden runs 6-inch InP . Zurich
runs 6-inch GaAs and is adding 6-inch InP for high-power lasers, while Fremont
runs a 3-inch line. GaAs capacity has grown fourfold since the 6-inch move about
six years ago, with capacity for over one billion VCSEL arrays per year. InP capacity
doubled last year, a quarter ahead of schedule, and management plans to double it

again or more.
Figure 4. COHR Capacity Expansion
Source: COHR at ECOC 2026
Shape, guide, and receive: Coherent grows garnet in the United States and says it
supplies a significant fraction of the industry's garnet and isolators, with
customers signing LTAs for capacity. The ELS is built on the transceiver line and is
sampling now. The rest of the portfolio covers specialty fiber (polarization-
maintaining fiber production-ready, mode-matched fiber introduced at ECOC,

multi-core fiber), micro-lens arrays, V-grooves, fiber array units, a detachable
connector, circulators, WDM multiplexers, and fiber shuffles.
Silicon photonics and engines: Management discussed Coherent having an in-
house silicon photonics design team, uses foundries for wafers, and does assembly
and test in-house. It ships 200G-per-lane silicon photonics in transceivers today
and showed a 400G link at OFC with a post-deadline paper. It has a 6.4T silicon

photonics NPO module (32 lanes of 200G) and a VCSEL-based NPO with 2D
arrays, which management says achieves about 1.2 pJ/bit including the laser.
Architecture trade-off: Management said NPO is easier to deploy and service but
costs power and signal integrity versus CPO, and that a common NPO socket can
take silicon photonics or VCSELs. It positioned Coherent as an honest advisor
because it can compare all the options. Chip-to-chip is a growing topic, with WDM

and circulators important there.

### Page 5

Coherent Corp (COHR.K)
21 September 2026 Citi Research
5
Figure 5. COHR CPO Offerings
Source: COHR at ECOC 2026
Figure 6. COHR NPO Offerings
Source: COHR at ECOC 2026
Key takeaways from Citi discussion with management:
What is Coherent's competitive advantage in 3.2T, and will it participate?
Management said it has already demonstrated the building blocks for the 3.2T
generation, including 400G-per-lane EMLs and 400G silicon photonics, and
expects to participate just as it enables 1.6T today. Management showed these

demonstrations as early as two years ago.
What is the gross margin path, and what offsets mix pressure? Management
mentioned that the 42%+ target was deliberately open-ended and that it expects
to exceed it. It cautioned against expecting a step change over the next two to
three quarters. Fast transceiver growth is a mix headwind, offset by overhead
absorption, yield gains and factory consolidation. Management expects the 800G-
to-1.6T transition to be margin-accretive, with the cutover forecast around 2028.

### Page 6

Coherent Corp (COHR.K)
21 September 2026 Citi Research
6
OCS, shipping for two to three quarters, is the first of the new platforms to
generate revenue. OCS, CPO and multi-rail will contribute more to margins as mix
builds.
What are the challenges and economics of the 6-inch InP transition?
Management said it announced 6-inch capability in March 2024 after years of
work with substrate vendors that began before the AI boom. Output is expected to
be roughly 50/50 between 3-inch and 6-inch by the end of the quarter, with all

incremental capacity on 6-inch, reaching about 75% by the end of 2027. A 6-inch
die costs about half as much per yielded die, and each line delivers about four
times the capacity. Yields are better on modern tooling. Management cited a three-
to-four-year lead over competitors and secured substrate supply from five
suppliers across three continents. It also credited its 6-inch GaAs VCSEL line to a
large consumer-electronics customer that demanded the larger wafer size.

Will Coherent keep external InP suppliers? COHR only sources EMLs externally
and that it will keep some external supply for continuity on a critical component.
How does Coherent differ from Chinese laser suppliers? Management argued
that module assembly is easier to replicate but high-speed lasers are not. It said
there are no high-speed EMLs or broadband tunable lasers from China, and that
Chinese CW lasers top out around 70-100 mW. It sees only one InP laser

competitor worth naming and expects new Chinese module entrants to take share
from existing Chinese suppliers rather than from Coherent.
Does NPO vs CPO matter for content, and can low-power lasers serve NPO?
Management said it does not matter much, because the components and dollar
content are similar. Designs are custom and take 18 to 24 months to reach volume,
and management reiterated scale-up production revenue in the second half of next
year. Management rejected the view that NPO can use low-power lasers, arguing

that coupling several weak lasers loses light and defeats the power-efficiency
purpose.
What is multi-rail, and how is it progressing? Multi-rail addresses scale-across
links between data centers. Management said it delivers four times the capacity in
the same footprint at roughly 30% lower power per lane, enabled by a multi-chip
uncooled pump that it says has no comparable competitor. Small shipments are
underway and production shipments begin in the first half of next calendar year. It

sells at the component, module, line-card and system level, including white-label
to OEMs.

### Page 7

Coherent Corp (COHR.K)
21 September 2026 Citi Research
7
Coherent Corp
Company description
COHR is a vertically integrated manufacturing company that develops
engineered materials and opto-electronic components for industrial
materials processing, communications, aerospace and defense, consumer
electronics, semiconductor capital equipment, life sciences and automotive
end markets. COHR produces a wide variety of application-specific photonic
and electronic materials and components, and deploys them in various forms,

including integration with advanced software. COHR's customer base
includes original equipment manufacturers, laser end users, system
integrators of high-power lasers, manufacturers of equipment and devices for
industrial, optical communications, consumer electronics, security and
monitoring applications, U.S. government prime contractors, and various U.S.
government agencies. COHR is a headquartered in Pennsylvania.
Investment strategy
We are Buy-rated on COHR. We believe that the stock's multiple will benefit

from the AI appeal while its fundamentals should expand on new
management's strong execution and focus on profitability.
Valuation
We value COHR at $420 or 30x forward P/E applied to FY2028 EPS of ~$16
discounted back. Our COHR valuation is nevertheless at a 10% discount to
the average forward P/E of AI optics peers to reflect a lower AI sales mix and
3-year EPS CAGR vs peers such as LITE.
Risks
Downside risks to the achievement of our target price include: 1) increased

competition on AI and SiC leads to limited market share growth and limited
revenue upside, 2) inability to achieve synergies and margin targets
established post acquisition of Finisar, and 3) prolonged economic recession
leads to slower 5G build-out and lower consumer demand driving revenue of
COHR down.
Upside risks to the achievement of our target price include: 1) gaining share in
AI and SiC market through new product offerings and first to market status

on larger wafer sizes, 2) quicker and stronger adoption of AI workloads,
electric and autonomous vehicles, and 3) quicker return of margins to pre-
Finisar acquisition levels.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do

——8 月经济数据解读
 8 月工业增加值当月同比 5.20%，预期 4.70%，前值 4.50%；8 月工业增加值
累计同比 5.30%，前值 5.30%；8 月固定资产投资完成额累计同比-7.20%，预

期-7.20%，前值-6.70%；8月社会消费品零售总额当月同比0.40%，预期0.70%，
前值 0.60%；8 月社会消费品零售总额累计同比 1.10%，前值 1.20%。我们解
读如下：
 一、国际油价上破 150美元/桶概率较低，明年 2 季度之后美联储加息概率不
大；
 二、8 月工业增加值数据总体表现尚可，但年内工业增加值当月同比或承压；
 三、房地产投资增速短期还将继续下探，房价同比“转正”时点是企稳“观
察锚”之一；
 四、城镇调查失业率上行，叠加美联储加息及 M2同比走弱，我国消费增速或
还将承压。
 风险提示：1.美伊战争持续时长超预期；2.美国通胀上行幅度高于预期。
 注：本文所有数据，均来自 WIND资讯，经英大证券整理。

### Page 3

宏观研究报告
请务必阅读最后一页的免责条款 3
事件：8 月工业增加值当月同比 5.20%，预期 4.70%，前值 4.50%；8 月工业增加值
累计同比 5.30%，前值 5.30%；8 月固定资产投资完成额累计同比-7.20%，预期-7.20%，
前值-6.70%；8月社会消费品零售总额当月同比 0.40%，预期 0.70%，前值 0.60%；8 月社
会消费品零售总额累计同比 1.10%，前值 1.20%。
解读：
一、国际油价上破 150美元/桶概率较低，明年 2季度之后美联储加息概率不大
国际油价剧烈波动是 2026年全球宏观经济面临的不确定性之一。当前国际油价位于
90-100美元/桶之间，主要支撑因素是美伊战争对原油供给的冲击，以及美国宏观经济在
2026年走强。在这两个因素的作用下，短期内国际油价还将位于一定高位。
当下资本市场关注的焦点之一是，本轮周期国际油价能否突破 150美元/桶。我们认
为国际油价突破 150美元/桶的概率较低。我们做出这一判断的依据来自需求与供给两个

方面。从需求端看：一是由于原油消费的刚性，短期之内国际油价上涨推升消费增速，
但是随着时间的推移，高油价对消费群体的收入形成较大压力，进而在中期反噬全球对
原油的需求，对国际油价形成利空；二是在 9 月美联储启动加息之后，后续还将继续加
息，而在加息之前美国联邦基金目标利率就已经位于相对高位，“高位加息”进一步推
升美国联邦基金目标利率，压制全球宏观经济，进而对国际油价形成利空。从供给端看：
一是波斯湾地区之外的原油生产，并未因为美伊战争封锁波斯湾而停止，相反，受全球
供给短期短缺以及连带的高油价的刺激，产油国大概率加大生产力度，以填补伊朗原油
缺位留下的全球供给缺口，这一点可以得到数据证实：4-8月，委内瑞拉原油月均产量在
100万桶的基础上不断上行，创 2019年 2 月以来新高，与此同时，美国原油月均产量持
续位于 1350万桶之上，且总体呈上行走势；二是随着时间的推移，海湾地区原油运输基
础设施不断建设与完善，体现在沙特阿拉伯的原油产量上：3月受美伊战争的影响，沙特
阿拉伯的原油产量从 2 月的 1011.2 万桶，快速下跌至 762.6万桶，在 4 月跌至 675.5 万

桶之后，总体呈上行走势，8 月录得 727.6 万桶。也就是说，随着时间的推移，伊朗对波
斯湾的封锁效应，以及连带的对国际油价的推升力度，大概率是减弱的。
图 1：委内瑞拉原油月均产量在 100 万桶的基础上不断上行（万桶）
数据来源：Wind资讯，英大证券整理
综上，我们认为在需求端中期边际收缩以及供给端中短期边际扩张的背景下，国际
油价在 2026 年 9 月至 2027年 12 月不具备冲击 150 美元/桶的潜力。鉴于国际油价走势

是美国 CPI同比的主导因素，因此在国际油价难以大幅冲高的假设下，我们认为 2027年
3月美国 CPI同比大概率承压下行，这将降低美联储加息的概率，增加美联储维持利率不
变的概率，并加大市场的降息预期。值得指出的是，根据历史经验，除非特殊情况，美
联储不会在加息结束后快速启动降息进程，叠加利率高位对美国宏观经济的压制效应需
要一段时间才能体现，尤其是美国 CPI同比可能会有一段时间的反复，因此美联储下一
次降息的时间点，可能位于 2027年 4季度或 4季度之后。
二、8 月工业增加值数据总体表现尚可，但年内工业增加值当月同比或承压
8月工业增加值数据总体表现尚可：一是 8月工业增加值当月同比录得 5.20%，高于
预期 0.50 个百分点，较前值大幅上行 0.70 个百分点；二是 8 月工业增加值累计同比录
得 5.30%，持平于前值，为 2024年 1 月以来低值；三是 8月工业增加值环比录得 0.54%，
较前值上行 0.43个百分点，创近 3 年同期新高。

分部类看，8 月采矿业、制造业与电力燃气及水的生产和供应业工业增加值当月同比
分别为-1.40%、6.10%、4.90%，分别较前值上行 2.80、0.60、-0.10 个百分点。其中：8
月制造业工业增加值当月同比较前值大幅上行0.60个百分点，创2025年10月以来新高，
是 8 月工业增加值当月同比的主要支撑项；8 月采矿业工业增加值当月同比虽然大幅上行
2.80 个百分点，是绝对读数依旧位于负值区间，且处于历史低位区间；8 月电力燃气及
水的生产和供应业工业增加值当月同比虽然较前值小幅下行，但绝对读数位于相对高位。
新动能方面，8月高技术产业工业增加值当月同比录得 16.70%，虽然较前值下行 0.20
个百分点，但是连续 7个月录得两位数增长，且为 2021年 9月以来次高值。与高技术产
业工业增加值当月同比相关联的一个指标是高技术制造业 PMI。从图形走势看，高技术制
造业 PMI 领先高技术产业工业增加值当月同比：高技术产业工业增加值当月同比的三个
低点分别是 2019 年 8 月的 6.10%、2020 年 10 月的 6.30%，以及 2023 年 7 月的 0.70%，

与之相对应，高技术制造业 PMI的三个低点分别是 2018年 12月的 48.10%、2020年 6 月
的 51.10，以及 2022年 12 月的 47.40，也就是说，高技术制造业 PMI 的三个低点分别领
先高技术产业工业增加值当月同比的三个低点 8 个月、4个月、7个月。
图 2：高技术制造业 PMI领先高技术产业工业增加值当月同比（%；%）
数据来源：Wind资讯，英大证券整理
在美联储加息的情况下，高技术制造业 PMI 是承压下行的，而在美联储降息的背景
下，高技术制造业 PMI 是上行的。当前美联储在高位加息，则高技术制造业 PMI大概率

会面临压力，进而对高技术产业工业增加值当月同比形成一定压力。当然，对高技术产
业工业增加值也面临利多。9月 26 日中美两国元首就中美建设性战略稳定关系及重大国
际地区问题深入交换意见，达成八点成果共识。其中，第七项与高技术产业直接相关，
即“双方同意建立中美人工智能对话，交流人工智能相关风险和惠益。下次对话将于今
年 11 月举行。双方同意建立人工智能事件的沟通渠道”，此外，第五项与高技术产业间
接相关，即“两国元首认可中美经贸磋商机制积极作用和双方经贸团队磋商成果，包括
建立并推进贸易理事会等机制，达成‘300亿美元’对等降税安排，延期吉隆坡经贸磋商
成果等，并指示予以落实。”值得指出的是，虽然高技术产业因为中美两国元首会晤面临
产业角度的利多，但是美联储加息使得高技术产业面临来自宏观角度的利空。我们认为，
宏观角度的利空可能大于产业角度的利多。因此，我们认为高技术制造业增加值当月同
比的上行速度，可能会有一定程度的放缓。在此背景下，叠加国内需求依旧疲弱，使得

年内工业增加值当月同比或承压。
三、房地产投资增速短期还将继续下探，企稳时点或可观察房价同比“转正”时点
8月房地产开发投资完成额累计同比为-19.90%，较前值下行 0.70个百分点，创 1999
年 2 月有数据记录以来新低，持续 49个月位于负值区间。
仅从销售端看，8 月商品房销售面积累计同比为-12.10%，较前值下行 0.30 个百分点，
持续 4 个月下探，表明 5 月至 8月商品房销售又出现一定的走弱。与此同时，8 月商品房
销售额累计同比录得-13.00%，连续 6 个月单边累计上行 7.20 个百分点，创年内新高，
但是要注意到，2026 年 1-8 月的整体情况，弱于 2025年全年的-12.60%。最后，8 月商
品房待售面积累计同比录得-1.10%，较前值下行 0.30个百分点，连续 6 个月位于负值区
间，创 2021年 6月以来新低，表明商品房的库存情况持续好转。综上，虽然面临商品房
待售面积累计同比进入负值区间的利多，但是商品房销售面积累计同比在负值区间深度

下探，预示房地产开发投资完成额累计同比在短期内还将继续下探。
以上判断得到来自居民端的佐证。从当月值的角度看，8 月居民户新增中长期贷款为
-822.00亿元，环比少减 380.00 亿元，同比多减 1,022.00亿元；从累计值的角度看，1-8
月居民户新增中长期贷款为 188.00 亿元。不论是当月值，还是累计值，均创有数据记录
以来的“新低”：8月-822.00亿元的读数，是 2007年-2026年同期的最低值，且是唯一
一个进入负值区间的月份；1-8月 188.00 亿元的读数，是 2007年-2026 年同期的最低值。
以上表明当前我国居民对房贷的需求较为疲弱。这一点也得到资金端的印证：房地产开
发资金来源中的个人按揭贷款累计同比在 8 月录得-22.40%，虽然较前值上行 1.10 个百
分点，但是连续 35个月位于负值区间。
目前的问题是，在房地产投资增速大概率还将继续承压的背景下，房价增速能否起
来？客观而言，在绝大多数时间里，房地产开发投资完成额累计同比与 70 大中城市二手

住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比是正相关的，且 70 大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数
当月同比领先房地产开发投资完成额累计同比，但是自 2024 年 10 月起，二者走势发生
了背离：70大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比在负值区间持续上行，但是房
地产开发投资完成额累计同比在负值区间持续下探。对这一现象的一个解释是，虽然 70
大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比上行，但是依旧位于负值区间，所以不能
有效带动房地产开发投资完成额累计同比企稳上行。一个假设是，如果 70 大中城市二手
住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比上行至正值区间，则房地产开发投资完成额累计同比有
企稳上行的可能。

图 3：房地产投资增速短期还将继续下探，企稳时点或可观察房价同比“转正”时点（%；%）

接下来的问题是，70大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比有无可能升至正
值区间？8 月该值的读数为-5.10%，创近 30 个月新高。从内部结构看，8 月一线城市、
二线城市、三线城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比分别录得-2.70%、-4.90%、
-5.60%，分别较前值上行 1.0、0.20、0.20 个百分点。其中，一线城市的读数最高，且
向“零值”趋近。按照这个趋势，一线城市的房价同比有可能“转正”。在一线城市房价
同比领先二线城市与三线城市的背景下，二线城市房价同比存在转正的可能。一旦二线
城市房价同比“转正”，则 70 大中城市二手住宅房屋销售价格指数当月同比大概率同时
“转正”，届时我们再观察房地产开发投资完成额累计同比是否能企稳。
四、城镇调查失业率上行，叠加美联储加息及 M2同比走弱，我国消费增速或还将承

压
8月主要失业率指标均不低于 7 月，表明 8月就业情况较 7 月出现边际走弱。具体看：
8 月城镇调查失业率录得 5.30%，较前值上行 0.10 个百分点，创近 5 个月新高；8 月 31
个大城市城镇调查失业率录得 5.30%，较前值上行 0.10个百分点，持平于 2026 年 3 月与
2025年8月，为2024年9月以来高点；8月本地户籍人口就业人员调查失业率录得5.30%，
较前值上行 0.10个百分点，创近 4 个月新高，为近 5 个月高点；8 月外来户籍人口就业
人员调查失业率录得 5.20%，持平于前值，为近 5 个月高点；8 月外来农业户籍人口就业
人员调查失业率录得 5.00%，较前值上行 0.10个百分点，创近 4 个月新高，为近 5 个月
高点。
分年龄组看，不含在校生的 8月 16-24岁劳动力、25-29岁劳动力、30-59 岁劳动力
城镇调查失业率分别录得 18.90%、7.50%、3.90%，分别较前值上行 1.0、0.30、0 个百分
点，其中，16-24岁劳动力城镇调查失业率持平于 2025年 8 月，为 2023年 12 月有数据

记录以来高点，25-29 岁劳动力城镇调查失业率创近 5 个月新高，而 30-59 岁劳动力城镇
调查失业率则处于历史低位区间。可以看出，初级劳动力面临更大的就业压力。
8月企业就业人员平均工作时间录得 48.20小时/周，连续 4个月持平于该值，为近
7个月高点。在城镇调查失业率系统性上升的背景下，企业就业人员平均工作时间依旧持
平于前值，表明在劳动力内部就业情况是冷热不均的：一部分人的工作时间上升，而另
一部分人的工作时间下降。从分年龄组城镇调查失业率的情况看，工作时间随着年龄的

请务必阅读最后一页的免责条款 7
增长而增加。这一现象对我国消费增速形成压力：相比 30-59 岁劳动力，16-24 岁劳动力
的工资收入低，且年轻人消费需求较为旺盛，边际消费倾向相对更高，也就是说，16-24
岁劳动力失业率持续上升，对消费增速形成更大的压力。
与 8 月城镇调查失业率系统性上升相对应，8 月消费数据系统性走弱。具体看：一是
8月社会消费品零售总额当月同比 0.40%，低于预期 0.30个百分点，较前值下行 0.20个
百分点，创近 4 个月新低；二是 8 月社会消费品零售总额累计同比 1.10%，较前值下行
0.10个百分点，创 2023年 2 月以来新低；三是 8 月社会消费品零售总额环比录得-0.13%，
较前值下行 0.14个百分点，时隔 2 个月再次进入负值区间，创近 4 个月新低。
我们认为，年内消费增速大概率还将承压。这一判断的依据，除了城镇调查失业率
上升之外，还来自两个方面。第一，年内美联储大概率至少再加息一次，这将对我国出
口增速形成一定程度的压制，尽管出口增速未必下行，进而对我国消费增速形成一定压

力，而从历史经验看，美联储加息利空我国宏观经济，进而利空我国消费增速。第二，8
月我国 M2 同比录得 7.50%，较前值下行 0.20 个百分点，创 2025年 4 月以来新低。这将
在一定程度上对我国消费增速形成压制。
图 4：M2同比创 2025年 4月以来新低，将在一定程度上对我国消费增速形成压制（%）
数据来源：Wind资讯，英大证券整理
9月 19日央行货币政策委员会召开 2026年第三季度例会。通稿删除第二季度例会的
“跨周期调节”表述，增加“综合运用并适时调整货币政策工具”，“不断完善工具设
计和管理”，“加快推动新旧动能转换”等表述。总体而言，第三季度例会表达出货币
政策“宽松”的倾向，这将对工业增加值增速、房地产投资增速以及消费增速形成支撑。

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月AI债发行突然“踩刹车”，大摩判断：四季度或卷土重来.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)

### Page 1

摩根⼠丹利将这⼀放缓定性为 " 暂停⽽⾮退潮 " ，预计四季度发⾏将回升，但不会重现上半年
的爆发式增⻓。该机构认为， 9 ⽉降温的原因并⾮基本⾯恶化或资本短缺 —— 前期发⾏⼤幅
前置、数据中⼼建设遭遇监管政治阻⼒、利率急升迫使项⽬层⾯条款重新谈判，三重因素
叠加。
摘要：
9 ⽉全球 AI 相关债券发⾏仅约 230 亿美元，为今年次低⽉份。美国投资级市场 AI 发⾏直接 " 挂零 " 。
摩根⼠丹利将这⼀放缓定性为 " 暂停⽽⾮退潮 " ，预计四季度发⾏将回升，但不会重现上半年的爆
发式增⻓。
截⾄ 9 ⽉底，今年全球 AI 相关债券发⾏总额已达 4660 亿美元，是去年全年 2160 亿美元的两倍以
上。
⼤摩在最新报告中分析称， 9 ⽉降温的原因并⾮基本⾯恶化或资本短缺 —— 前期发⾏⼤幅前置、
数据中⼼建设遭遇监管政治阻⼒、利率急升迫使项⽬层⾯条款重新谈判，三重因素叠加。
9 ⽉为何降温
今年 AI 融资的节奏极不均匀。 6 ⽉以 1130 亿美元的单⽉发⾏量创下全年峰值，随后逐⽉下⾏。

⾼质量超⼤规模云⼚商（⾕歌、亚⻢逊、 Meta 、微软）今年在投资级市场的发⾏额达约 1320 亿
美元，同⽐增⻓约 25 倍；加上甲⻣⽂和 SpaceX ，六⼤超⼤规模⼚商全⼝径（含⾮美元币种）发
⾏已达约 2540 亿美元，⼤量集中在上半年。摩根⼠丹利指出， " 经历了繁忙的夏季后，考虑到已
完成的发⾏量以及发⾏⼈建⽴发⾏节奏的努⼒，我们预期近期供给会放缓。 "
9 ⽉美国投资级市场 AI 发⾏为零。⾮美元市场略有活动，亚⻢逊发⾏了⼀笔 42.5 亿英镑的四批次
交易。杠杆融资⽅⾯，软银集团发⾏了约 100 亿美元⾼收益债券， AI 数据中⼼运营商 Crusoe 发⾏
了约 5 亿美元贷款， 9 ⽉杠杆融资总额约 140 亿美元。
第⼆重因素是监管和政治层⾯的约束。新数据中⼼建设⾯临许可证、电⼒供应和政治阻⼒ ——
摩根⼠丹利此前将其总结为 " 三个 P" ：⼈员（ people ）、电⼒（ power ）和政治（ politics ），这些
约束正在从潜在⻛险变为现实制约。
第三重因素是利率。收益率⼤幅上⾏使得项⽬层⾯的交易可能需要重新谈判，低评级发⾏⼈和

项⽬融资对资⾦成本更为敏感。
四季度展望：超⼤规模⼚商将回归，但节奏不同于去年
摩根⼠丹利预计四季度发⾏将超过 9 ⽉⽔平，但不会像去年那样集中爆发 —— 去年⼤部分供给出
现在四季度。
⾼质量超⼤规模云⼚商预计将重返美国投资级市场，同时继续在⾮美元市场融资。今年⾮美元
超⼤规模发⾏已达约 720 亿美元，接近全球超⼤规模发⾏总量的三分之⼀，币种从去年单⼀的美
元和欧元扩展⾄加元、英镑、瑞郎、澳元和⽇元。摩根⼠丹利预计四季度还将有欧元交易⼊
场。

写评论
数据中⼼项⽬融资的节奏更难预测。摩根⼠丹利将今年 ABS 和 CMBS 的发⾏预估下调⾄ 250-300
亿美元，意味着四季度还有约 50-100 亿美元的供给空间。
四季度回升的⼀个重要推动⼒是 2027 年资本开⽀预期。摩根⼠丹利对六⼤超⼤规模⼚商 2027 年
合计资本开⽀预测约为 1.4 万亿美元，显著⾼于市场共识的约 1-1.1 万亿美元。三季报（预计 10 ⽉
陆续发布）可能带来⼜⼀轮资本开⽀上修，直接驱动融资需求。
摩根⼠丹利认为，⾼质量超⼤规模⼚商对⾼利率 " 在很⼤程度上不敏感 "—— 投⼊资本回报率
（ ROIC ）前景强劲，债务融资成本仍低于股权融资。这些⼚商整体杠杆率仅 1.3 倍（净杠杆 0.4
倍），现⾦ / 债务⽐率 132% ，信⽤评级中位数 AA- ，资产负债表空间充裕。

宏观考验⼤于供给冲击
摩根⼠丹利强调，对投资级信⽤市场⽽⾔， " 宏观环境 —— ⽽⾮供给过剩 —— 是年底前最⼤的考
验。 "
投资级债券年内总回报已下跌 3% ，季度回报下跌 4% ，回撤幅度已达到可能触发共同基⾦和 ETF
⼤规模赎回的⽔平。 10 年期美债收益率从 1% 升⾄ 5% 以上，过去五年多次利率重定价对信⽤利
差和资⾦流造成了反复冲击。
不过，摩根⼠丹利认为信⽤市场整体能够消化收益率上⾏，因为名义增⻓率维持在 6% 以上、企
业盈利增速更强，利率上⾏有坚实的基本⾯⽀撑。该⾏经济学家预计美联储仅再加息两次，⽐
市场定价的接近四次更偏鸽派。
信⽤分化⽅⾯，摩根⼠丹利重申偏好有担保资产（约占 AI 相关债务的 30% ）。数据中⼼有担保债
券的利差波动主要由建设⻛险驱动 —— 许可证延迟、电⼒供应、租约不确定性，已稳定运营的
ABS 和 CMBS 资产受此类冲击较⼩。芯⽚融资的资产属性允许更快的现⾦流⽣成和摊还，但⽬前
公开交易的产品仍然有限。
展望四季度之后，摩根⼠丹利认为超⼤规模⼚商利差压缩交易的⼤部分⾏情可能已经⾛完：发

⾏节奏正趋于可预测，资本开⽀正向芯⽚等短久期资产转移， AI 投资的强劲回报也在持续验证⽀
出逻辑的稳健性。

Document 159: 9月新增就业料降至9万：今夜非农真正的“炸点”，或在8月数据是否大幅下修

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月新增就业料降至9万：今夜非农真正的“炸点”，或在8月数据是否大幅下修.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

华尔街普遍预计 9 ⽉新增就业将回落⾄ 9 万⼈，失业率维持 4.1% 。 8 ⽉强劲数据主要受异常
季节性因素提振，其修正幅度将直接决定 9 ⽉数据的强弱观感。随着美联储官员近期表态令
10 ⽉加息概率骤降⾄ 25% ，市场焦点转向⻓端利率。⽬前系统性基⾦持有约 3900 亿美元全
球债券空头，若就业数据意外爆冷，将引发剧烈的市场动荡。
摘要：
美国 9 ⽉⾮农就业报告将于周五（ 10 ⽉ 2 ⽇）公布，这是 10 ⽉ 28 ⽇美联储议息会议前最后⼀份就
业数据，市场对其影响⼒的定价却降⾄近期低点 —— ⽽历史经验表明，市场最不在意的时候，
往往是数据最能搅动市场的时候。
华尔街中位数预期为新增就业 9 万⼈，较 8 ⽉的 16.2 万⼤幅回落。市场⾼度关注 8 ⽉数据是否会被
⼤幅下修，因异常的季节性调整此前显著夸⼤了整体就业表现，这使得 9 ⽉的数据⾯临极⼤的基
数效应不确定性。
与此同时，美联储政策预期在过去⼀周已发⽣剧烈转向：本周⼀市场对 10 ⽉加息的定价约为
70% ，但在纽约联储主席威廉姆斯表态 " 不急于加息 " 、 8 ⽉核⼼ PCE 温和落地后，⾄周四收盘，

10 ⽉加息概率已跌⾄约 25% ，⾼盛将下次加息预期推迟⾄ 12 ⽉。
季节性调整是本次报告最⼤的不确定性来源。巴克莱测算，若以今年的季节性因⼦重新调整 8 ⽉
数据， 16.2 万的 " 亮眼 " 增幅将变为下降 7.4 万，意味着 8 ⽉数据存在严重⾼估。这⼀背景下， 8 ⽉
数据是否被下修，将直接决定 9 ⽉数据的解读⽅向。
对市场⽽⾔，真正的⻛险或许不在利率前端，⽽在⻓端债券。⾼盛数据显⽰， CTA 趋势策略基⾦
⽬前持有约 3900 亿美元全球债券空头，美国 10 年期国债空头仓位处于历史最⼤值的 99% ， 30 年
期则已达 100% 。⼀旦⾮农数据偏弱或失业率升⾄ 4.2% ，系统性基⾦的⼤规模空头回补可能引发
债市剧烈波动。
预期数字：共识偏低，分歧显著
华尔街 80 家机构的预测区间从巴克莱的 +5 万到野村的 +13 万不等，⼏乎所有机构的预测均低于 8
⽉读数，多数低于共识。

### Page 2

核⼼预期数据如下：
⾼盛预期新增就业 +8 万，略低于共识但⾼于三个⽉均值，同时将失业率预测下调⾄ 4.0% ，理由
是持续申领失业救济⼈数下降。⾼盛还预期平均时薪仅环⽐ +0.2% ，引⽤ " 不利的⽇历效应 " 作为
解释。
野村以 +13 万居华尔街最⾼，理由是 8 ⽉历史上是初值最容易被上修的⽉份。
⾮农就业： +9 万（前值 +16.2 万）； 3 个⽉均值 7.1 万， 6 个⽉均值 10.7 万， 12 个⽉均值 5 万
私⼈就业： +8.1 万（前值 +12.7 万）
失业率： 4.1% （前值 4.14% ，未四舍五⼊）
劳动参与率： 61.6% （与前值持平）
平均时薪：环⽐ +0.3% ，同⽐ +3.2% （前值 +3.1% ）
平均⼯时： 34.3 ⼩时（前值 34.4 ⼩时）

### Page 3

失业率与薪资：细节决定市场反应
失业率预测区间为 4.0% ⾄ 4.2% ，分歧根源在于 8 ⽉未四舍五⼊的 4.14% 。
薪资⽅⾯，⾼盛和野村均预期环⽐ +0.2% ，德意志银⾏则⾼于共识，预期 +0.4% 。⾼盛更⼴泛的
薪资追踪指标显⽰同⽐ +3.5% ，第三季度年化 +3.1% 。 Wolfe Research 指出，薪资增速仍 " 低于
美联储偏好的 3.5%-4.0% 区间 " ，称之为 " 出⼈意料的温和 " 。
季节性调整： 8 ⽉ " 虚胖 " ， 9 ⽉存在双向⻛险
季节性因⼦是本次报告最核⼼的解读难点。
Wolfe Research 指出，在典型的 8 ⽉，季节性调整通常会将季调后数字压低 10 万以上。但今年 8
⽉，季节性因⼦反⽽推⾼了数据 —— 这是⾃ 2021 年以来⾸次出现这⼀情况。
⾼盛和野村预期 4.0% ，依据是持续申领⼈数下降；
Wolfe Research 预期 4.17% ，四舍五⼊后为 4.2% ；
美银预期 4.1% ，但警告 8 ⽉家庭就业调查激增 56.9 万后存在回调⻛险，可能将失业率推⾄

4.2% ；并补充 “ 即便 4.2% 也与健康的劳动⼒市场基本⾯相符 ” ；
德意志银⾏警告，劳动参与率若略有上升，即可能导致失业率四舍五⼊⾄ 4.2% 。

### Page 4

美银经济学家 Shruti Mishra 给出了最清晰的解释：
美银将这⼀异常归因于调查周期差异 ——2026 年 8 ⽉为四周调查区间，⽽ 2024 年和 2025 年均为
五周。
巴克莱的结论形成了⼀个清晰的逻辑链：
美银建议投资者 “ 不要被标题数字迷惑 ” ，并将潜在就业增⻓估算维持在 “10 万 +” 的健康⽔平。
8 ⽉未季调就业增幅实际上低于去年同期，但今年的季节性调整 " 接近于零 " ，⽽ 2025 年 8 ⽉的调
整幅度为 -17.8 万，导致今年的未季调增幅⼏乎全部流⼊季调后数字。
若 8 ⽉数据被下修  → 9 ⽉数据可能意外偏强；若 8 ⽉数据未被下修  → 9 ⽉数据可能偏弱。

### Page 5

此外，美银还提⽰⼀项潜在下⾏⻛险：约 20 万名海地 TPS 持有者于 7 ⽉ 27 ⽇失去⼯作授权，主要
集中在餐饮、医疗、运输和零售⾏业。美银基准情景为渐进式拖累，但承认 “9 ⽉数据受到的冲击
可能超出预期 ” 。
劳动⼒市场⾼频指标：整体偏正⾯，消费者信⼼是异类
多项⾼频指标显⽰劳动⼒市场仍具韧性：
唯⼀的反向信号来⾃消费者信⼼调查。世界⼤型企业联合会调查中， “ 就业充裕 ” 与 “ 就业难找 ” 之
差收窄⾄仅 +1.7 ，六个⽉就业预期净值降⾄ -14.4 ，暗⽰消费者感知到的就业市场仍在⾛弱。
美联储政策：从 " 跳过 " 到 " 加息 " 的路径
在三年来⾸次加息后，美联储点阵图中位数预⽰ 2026 年还有⼀次加息。市场⼀度将其定价在 10
⽉，但随后迅速撤退。
⽬前市场共识已转向 “ 跳过 10 ⽉、 12 ⽉加息 ” 。⾼盛经济学家认为，由于预计核⼼ PCE 年底将降
⾄ 3.0% （低于美联储 3.4% 的预测）， “FOMC 最终得出⽆需进⼀步加息结论的可能性相当⼤ ” 。

巴克莱同样预期 10 ⽉暂停、 12 ⽉加息。德意志银⾏基准情景为 12 ⽉和明年 3 ⽉各加息⼀次。

的游戏规则改变者，市场将更关注 CPI 数据” 。
初请失业⾦：调查参考周为 19.8 万，低于 8 ⽉调查窗⼝的 20.7 万；持续申领⼈数降⾄ 171.9 万，

领涨

本不在乎。 ”

如果数据 “ 过冷 ” （新增不⾜ 2 万⼈或失业率升⾄ 4.2% 以上），不仅加息预期将被彻底抹除，还
将引爆 CTA 债券空头的全⾯轧空。

Document 177: A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader (2026-10-06)

Source PDF: all report pdf/A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader (2026-10-06).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 12
Status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #interestrates #businessconditions #credit
A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
10:43 September 18 2026 Share
Business investment is now in the driver’s seat on growth, implying credit growth is a less
reliable guide to financial conditions more broadly.
Strong business investment, autonomous from government or central bank policy, has filled the gap
left by slower growth in public demand, and changed the narrative around the growth cycle. This has

implications for how we should think about financial conditions, and specifically business credit

### Page 2

Historically, much of the commentary on the growth cycle in Australia has been policy-driven. The RBA
sets the cash rate, which affects housing and mortgage markets, and so consumer spending. Business
investment was seen as largely downstream of a consumer-driven domestic demand cycle, on the logic
that businesses will not invest if demand is not there.

There were exceptions to this, most notably the rise of China and associated mining investment boom.
The pervasive effects of this boom and its unwind were systematically underestimated. Also not fully
appreciated at the time was the deadening effect of risk aversion and weak investment appetite in the
wake of the GFC. But these factors mostly played out over a longer horizon, and the shorter-run rates–
consumer nexus tended to be more in focus.

Another policy-driven narrative became more prominent in recent years, with public demand as the
swing variable. Government infrastructure projects and the growth in the care economy were driving
growth in domestic demand. Initially we worried that when the infrastructure projects ended and the
expansion in the care economy matured, the handover would be ‘shaky’ and growth momentum would
weaken further.

Fortunately, that did not happen. Developments in the private sector, autonomous from domestic policy,
have recently become a key driver and filled the gap as public sector demand growth slowed. In many
respects, the data centre boom and associated renewables spend is rewriting the narrative, and our
recent forecast revisions reflect this.

This shift has several implications for how we should think about financial conditions and specifically
how business credit growth fits into any assessment of financial conditions.

First, business investment is not that correlated with short-term interest rates. This was true in
the post-GFC period of slow growth, when hurdle rates stayed high even though interest rates were
unusually low. It will also be true in the upswing. The hyperscalers and other funders that are driving
the data centre boom are essentially indifferent to cost. They do not seem to care that they are funding
themselves at much wider spreads than similarly rated non-tech firms. Nor do they seem to care about
cost to build. They just want to get it done as fast as possible. But if their activities are impervious to
growth.
Typically, business investment is only loosely correlated with interest rates, and business credit
growth is in turn only loosely correlated with new investment. Business balance sheets strengthened
in the wake of the pandemic, so there might be scope for further increases in leverage. The pre-
pandemic period of low growth, inflation and interest rates is in any case not the relevant period of
comparison for where we are now.
If something is essentially impervious to interest rates, then it cannot be an indicator of financial
conditions more broadly. There is a difference between a claim that policy is restrictive but needs to
be even tighter because of a positive shock, and a claim that policy is not tight at all. These are
important considerations for how we should interpret developments in business credit.

### Page 3

interest rates and spreads, they cannot guide views on how the economy is responding to interest
rates.

Second, business investment is only loosely correlated with business credit quarter to quarter or
year to year. A stylised description of many businesses’ strategies is to use debt funding to acquire
assets or other companies, but use retained earnings to fund new investment. Short-run fluctuations in
business credit growth might therefore provide weak or misleading signals about investment or activity
more broadly. Moreover, if businesses’ funding mix changes, even medium-term shifts in business
credit growth might have little implication for underlying activity.

Third, balance sheets matter. Businesses came out of COVID cashed up, in part because of the fiscal
support they received during the pandemic. Business leverage – whether measured as debt relative to
financial assets broadly or debt to cash plus deposits – dipped below even the low post-GFC average.
It has since returned to that average but might have scope to increase further. This would not
necessarily mean credit supply has eased. Rather, the population of borrowers has effectively changed
and become stronger.

Fourth, and related to the previous point, the comparison period matters. We must recall that the
period between the GFC and the pandemic was the anomaly – low growth, low inflation, low
investment – and extremely low interest rates. The global structure of interest rates is likely to be higher
now and into the future, because of the AI boom, the US fiscal situation and the end of the post-GFC
inflation undershoot. But this is not news, and it is not necessarily a sign of a change in financial
conditions more recently.

This point is particularly salient for total credit growth as an indicator of financial conditions. Even a
return to inflation around or above target, rather than consistently below as in the 2010s, means that
average nominal credit growth rates from that period are not representative of “normal”. Nominal
growth in the economy was slower, and nominal credit growth is downstream from that. Moreover,
many of the factors that made the post-GFC period unusual – the weak risk appetite, regulatory
adjustments and balance sheet repair – would be expected to weigh on credit growth especially
heavily. There is also a cyclical element here: more (real) credit growth might not be saying anything
about credit supply, and so “financial conditions”. Rather, stronger growth makes for stronger borrowers
that are more willing to demand credit and more qualified to access it.

All of this suggests that observers should be cautious in how they interpret business credit conditions.
If something is essentially impervious to interest rates, then it cannot be an indicator of
financial conditions more broadly. To be fair, the hyperscalers are issuing bonds, not borrowing from
the domestic banking system and being recorded in business credit. To the extent that current business
investment and credit growth are being driven by the data centre boom’s broader spillovers to other
sectors, though, that provides almost no signal about the tightness of monetary policy.

### Page 4

Related articles
This is important given the apparent tendency of some recent RBA and other commentary to conflate a
shock with a reason to revise the neutral rate higher. For example, in one recent speech, it was argued
that, because AI was a smaller shock for Australia than some other countries (debateable), long yields
would rise by less than elsewhere. This in turn would result in a lower exchange rate than otherwise,
necessitating higher interest rates. But the exchange rate depreciation is part of the transmission of
that differential response to different-sized shocks. It is not an independent outcome requiring that
interest rates need to be higher, actually. In the face of a positive shock, yes, policy rates would need to
be higher than if the shock had not occurred. But being a lesser beneficiary of that shock is not a
heads-I-hike-tails-your-rates-go-up double-whammy.

This framing is even more curious, when one considers that the RBA never took any signal from the
USD selloff earlier in the year. That selloff had nothing to do with conditions in Australia, but it surely
lifted the AUD exchange rate at the margin – at least against some trading partners – and therefore
was slightly disinflationary.

The broader lesson here is not that this time is different, but rather “last time” – the period between the
GFC and the pandemic – was different. It has also taken a while to normalise from the pandemic itself,
especially where balance sheets and financial behaviour are concerned. If we fail to understand this
recovery away from the post-GFC era, or the implications of more fleeting shocks for financial
behaviour, we risk misconstruing where the economy might be headed and where policy settings need
to be to achieve desired outcomes. The strength of business credit does not have the same
implications as similarly strong household credit would.

The boom in data centres and renewables is reshaping Australia’s economic narrative and needs to be
a consideration in policy settings. But care must be taken to avoid overstating its implications for
financial conditions. There are many good reasons to think that the long-run average for the global
structure of interest rates (and so neutral rates) will be higher in future than pre-pandemic. Current
strong growth in business credit is not necessarily one of them.

Document 189: Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 11
Status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• The ongoing oil-price shock shouldn’t prove structural, with flows likely to normalize in 
2027. But several pressure points are pulling oil and gas prices in different directions 
and the balance will decide where prices go next.  Brent jumped to nearly 110 USD/bbl 
after Saudi Arabia was forced to shut its East-West pipeline, pushing the net hit to Hormuz 
flows to about 4.3mbpd vs pre -war levels.  For now, pressure points (including strategic 
reserves near historic lows  and an underinvested refining base) are outweighing the two 
relief valves (shadow traffic in Hormuz and demand destruction). As we expect the Saudi 
pipeline to be repaired rather swiftly and volumes steady in Hormuz,  Brent should average 
87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before consolidating to 76 USD/bbl in 2027. But prices would hold 
near 100 USD/bbl if the outage extends toward six weeks and Hormuz remains in flux.  
• European gas prices are climbing as the region races to refill, though the resulting 
macro risk looks contained for now. Gas prices have rallied from the mid-40 EUR/MWh in 
July to over 80 EUR/MWh as Hormuz LNG loadings stay stuck at 15-25% of pre-war levels. 
We expect TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh in Q4 2026 before easing to 36 EUR/MWh in 2027 
amid larger supply from both the US and the Middle  East. A sustained move above 120  
EUR/MWh for six months would risk tipping the Eurozone into recession.  
• A separate refining and export bottleneck is keeping refined product prices elevated. 
US and European crack spreads have surged to 62 EUR/bbl and 74 EUR/bbl. On top of 
Hormuz disruptions, a significant share of Russia’s refining capacity has been  hit by 
Ukrainian strikes, resulting in  a diesel export ban. Until June, China also had export 
restrictions in place amid fear s of supply shortage s. A structurally underinvested refining 
base is adding to the multi -layered crisis . As a result,  global oil demand is expected to 
contract 2.5mbpd in 2026 as demand from Asia has collapsed. 
• Diesel-reliant transport and agriculture, Asian petrochemicals and fertilizers are the 
most exposed sectors  with very few short -term substitutes available , while producers 
with domestic feedstock flexibility are comparatively insulated.  Between March and 
August, fossil -fuel importers paid an estimated extra USD330bn. High d iesel prices are 
hitting trucking, agriculture and construction, and the Gulf’s 40% share of global sulfur 
exports is impacting food production via fertilizer s. The n ear-term adjustment is mainly 
through inventory drawdowns and output cuts rather than genuine substitution.  
• Households are only starting to feel the full inflationary hit, and the pass -through will 
remain sticky into 2027.  In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of August’s CPI 
increase, with pump prices set to reach 3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y, about 355 USD 
more per household annually). In Europe, regulated tariffs are catching up with a lag and 
lower-income households remain disproportionately exposed.  
• Bonds are already pricing  in most of the  energy premium, but sustained inflation and 
oil-price spikes could lead to further rate stress. Much of the initial repricing has already 
occurred. We estimate that a further increase in Brent to 120 USD/bbl would only lift yields 
by 5-10bps (vs 17-18bps on inflation swaps and 8-11bps on expected policy-rate pricing). 
This suggests that a significant further rise in yields would likely require a renewed large 
supply shock or evidence that inflation is delaying monetary -policy normalization. 
• Looking ahead, over the longer run, the unfolding events  are fundamentally a 
Hormuz/Gulf shock  that will reshape global energy  markets. The conflict is reshaping 
global energy trade routes: Gulf states are building out bypass infrastructure (ADCOP, the 
Kirkuk-Ceyhan/Development Road corridor, Duqm) while China, India, Japan and South 
Korea diversify away from Gulf -transiting barrels; LNG has no equivalent bypass, so gas 
exposure remains structural. 
Ludovic Subran

Allianz Research | 16 September 2026 
Chokepoints and pressure points : what is breaking 
and reshaping global energy markets

### Page 2

2

Roller-coaster oil markets  
The recent attack on Saudi Arabia’s East -West pipeline (Petroline), combined with stalled talks between Iran 
and Gulf states on Hormuz shipping, is compounding risks, but we expect the shock to fade. Brent has spiked to 
nearly 110  USD/bbl, widening an already -significant Persian Gulf supply gap (see Figure 1): despite lower Gulf 
production, exports proved more resilient, but the net hit to Hormuz flows still stands at about 4.3mbpd versus pre -
war levels. It remains uncertain when the S audi pipeline will resume normal flows of 7mbpd - a mild scenario sees 
volumes ramp back to 5-6mbpd within weeks, while a worst case keeps the pipeline shut for about six week but we 
see this as a temporary shock either way.

OECD commercial stocks remain the main shock absorber, but that buffer is thinning fast. US Strategic Petroleum 
Reserve draws have slowed to just 0.3mbpd over the past month - reserves are now near their lowest level in 
decades, so Washington has little scope to repeat the scale of releases seen in 2022  or earlier this year even if the 
deficit widens further ; while Japan and South Korea are rebuilding their own reserves rather than drawing them 
down. China’s crude imports, meanwhile, are only just restarting after months of weakness and remain about 
4mbpd below year-ago levels; refiners are taking advantage of firm margins and spare quota to buy and reprocess 
crude for re-export rather than to meet stronger domestic demand, so this is not yet a genuine demand recovery. 
Against this backdrop, we forecast Brent averaging 88 USD/bbl in Q3 and 87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before easing to 
76 USD/bbl in 2027 - but a deeper or more prolonged Saudi export disruption, without an offsetting reserve release, 
would keep the market in deficit for longer and raise the probability of Brent holding near 100  USD/bbl.

A natural gas squeeze looming over Europe? 
Natural gas prices are ticking up as Europe races to refill storage.  European gas is exposed to the same Middle 
East disruption through a different channel: LNG loadings through Hormuz have remained stuck at 15 -25% of pre-
war levels for two months, even as Qatar sends more empty tankers into the Gulf. We expect Qatari and Emirati 
LNG exports to normalize only in Q1 2027, so for TTF to keep pricing enough Asian LNG demand out of the market 
to free cargoes for Europe, prices must stay elevated for longer: the rally from the mid -40 EUR/MWh in July to over 
80 EUR/MWh has already cut some demand from the rest of the world. With Europe still playing catch -up on 
storage (see Figure 2), we expect TTF to average 65 EUR/MWh in both Q3 and Q4 2026 before consolidating to 36 
EUR/MWh in 2027 as Hormuz flows normalize and incremental US LNG supply arrives. The key near -term risk is 
storage: a cold winter combined with a lower-than-usual fill could deplete storage before spring, front-loading price 
pressure into early 2027.

### Page 4

For now, the macro risk from gas prices looks contained.  In 2022, gas prices averaged close to 130 EUR/MWh for 
a full year, yet the Eurozone still grew about 3.4%, cushioned by a post -pandemic demand boom and a fiscal 
response worth an estimated 0.4pp of growth. Today’s backdrop is less forgiving: fiscal space has narrowed and 
household demand is far less dynamic. We think TTF in the 80 -100 EUR/MWh range could be absorbed for nine 
months or more without a growth shock, but a sustained move above 120 EUR/MWh for six months or longer would 
tip the Eurozone into recession. Our base case, TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh through Q3 and Q4 2026, does not test 
that threshold, though a winter upside scenario would keep the tail risk alive.  
Refined products to remain expensive  
A two -layer infrastructure bottleneck is hitting refined products and will keep driving regional prices up, 
independent of crude.  In August, Brent averaged 89  USD/bbl, yet clean -product crack spreads surged to record 
levels, the US benchmark reached 62  EUR/bbl and European diesel crack 74 EUR/bbl (68% and 92% above their 
five-year averages) showing the main story is refining availability, not crude input costs. Three regions are driving 
the crunch. In the Middle East, the US -Iran conflict and Hormuz blockade have hit both refinery operations and 
product exports: pre -conflict flows of roughly  6mbpd through the Strait have fallen to 1.1mbpd, and the Saudi 
pipeline attack compounds this by also halting flows to the Yanbu refineries. In Russia, Ukrainian strikes cut refinery 
runs to multi -year lows by mid -May, and Moscow’s diesel export ban - extended through September, with no 
guarantee against further extension - has squeezed a market that depends on Russia for 11% of global diesel. In 
China, export restrictions imposed in March to preserve domestic inventories cut clean -product exports by 24.3% 
year-on-year over the first five months, though Beijing’s June release of 13mn tons should ease global supply, lifting 
China’s exports full-year total to 32mn tons, 0.6% above last year. 
Figure 3: US and Europe crack spread historical prices (USD/bbl)

Sources: 
 LSEG Workspace
 , Allianz Research

Global clean-product inventories are drying up as the crisis extends through 2026.  European (ARA) inventories 
are well below the five -year average. In the US, refiners are running at record utilization (98% in September), but 
most of the extra output is being exported for higher margins rather than sold domestically — gasoline stocks are 
6% and diesel 14% below seasonal norms, and US diesel topped 6  USD/gallon for the first time. Rising domestic 
prices could eventually pull barrels back into the US market and curb these exports, with some early – tough strongly 
pushed back by government officials for now  - talks about potential diesel export restrictions to support domestic 
supply. Additionally, there is little relief from spare capacity: years of underinvestment and refinery closures have 
left the West structurally unprepared, global refining investment is on track to fall below 40  USD bn this year (-15% 
y/y), and capacity is set to grow only marginally through 2040. Unlike 2022, when Europe and Asia reallocated 
capital toward LNG regasification within months, refining capacity cannot be added quickly enough to ease this 
shortage in the short or medium term, and rebuilding inventories will take time even once Hormuz flows normalize. 
We now expect world oil demand to contract by 2.5mbpd in 2026 as high product prices force further demand 
destruction, concentrated in middle distillates and petrochemical feedstocks in Asia. Longer term, persistently 
expensive products should accelerate substitution toward EVs and heat pumps, and put a premium on refineries 
with the flexibility to shift crude grades and product mix.

Downstream sector impact and potential substitution 
After six months, duration, and not just the peak price, is now the central macro risk. Between March and August, 
fossil-fuel importers paid an estimated 330  USD bn more than pre-war forward prices implied, split roughly evenly 
between crude and refined products - a real income transfer from importers to exporters that squeezes household 
purchasing power and corporate margins, weakens currencies, and strains public finances wherever governments 
subsidize fuel. The Hormuz crisis is unusual in disrupting many linked -but-hard-to-substitute commodities at once 
(crude, gas, LPG, naphtha and sulfur ) spilling into fertilizer and petrochemical shortages. Substitution can happen 
at three levels : (i) switching energy carriers, (ii) switching industrial feedstocks, or (iii) consumers switching final 
products. With the first working fastest but simply redistributing scarcity, and the other two developing more slowly 
but reshaping industrial structure permanently. Inflation itself spreads in three stages: a fast first -round hit to 
energy-intensive industries (speed depending on taxes and subsidies), a slower production -cost effect as old-price 
inventories and contracts run out, and a second -round effect as wages, inflation expectations and price -setting 
behavior adjust. 
The immediate impact is on energy -intensive industries.  Gulf states are major exporters of both fuels and 
feedstocks: Europe is less directly exposed to Gulf crude than East Asia but still feels it through global oil prices, LNG 
competition and product shortages; the US is more insulated on crude but exposed via global product prices, hitting 
lower-income consumers hardest while producers and refiners gain from higher margins. East and South Asia face 
the largest physical exposure, with China, India and Japan recording the highest net additional fossil -fuel import 
costs since the conflict began – USD 31.7 bn, USD 14.4bn and USD 10.4bn respectively. Diesel is the most powerful 
transmission channel given its role in trucking, agriculture, mining and construction - sectors that are hard to 
electrify, especially for long -haul transport and in emerging markets - while shipping and aviation face higher 
bunker/jet-fuel costs and rerouting. Among industrial inputs, East Asian petrochemical crackers reliant on imported 
naphtha are most exposed, whereas the US benefits from domestic ethane and China retains partial cover via coal-
based chemicals; metals, cement and glass are exposed through electricity and high-temperature heat costs, hitting 
aluminum hardest. Gulf countries supply more than 40% of global sulfur exports and about 20% of 
ammonia/nitrogen-fertilizer exports, so the crisis strikes food production through fertilizer, diesel, irrigation and 
packaging costs simultaneously. 
The second-round effect on the broader economy will be felt with a lag and last longer.  Food shows this best: 
packaged food embeds fertilizer, farm fuel, irrigation, refrigeration, processing heat, packaging, transport and 
labor, each repricing at a different speed : fresh produce reacts within weeks, grain can take up to a year via fertilizer 
and harvest effects, and restaurant prices adjust slowest but most persistently as labor and rent costs catch up. 
Large retailers can hedge and absorb costs better than small one s, and real household income falls as consumers 
trade down and defer spending. Export restrictions, subsidies and price controls can temporarily shield domestic 
food prices but tend to redistribute scarcity and worsen the burden on lower -income, food-importing economies.

### Page 6

6

Construction and real estate are hit both directly (cement, plastics, metals, diesel) and, more durably, indirectly 
through higher interest rates raising financing costs and lowering the present value of future rents. Compared with 
2022, the economy enters this shock with more renewable capacity and European LNG infrastructure - renewables 
installed since 2020 reportedly avoided USD 36bn of fossil -fuel imports in the first five months of the crisis - but 
public debt is higher and buffers are thinner after the pandemic and the 2022 crisis.  
Substitution will spread across fuels, feedstocks and products but initially redistributes inflation rather than 
eliminating it. The first response is inventory drawdowns, switching to costlier available inputs, output cuts and cost 
pass-through. Over time it becomes more structural, reorganizing downstream markets around flexibility, lower 
energy intensity and redundancy - persistently expensive oil and gas improve the economics of electrification, even 
though electrification changes commodity dependence rather than removing it. Because capital, regulatory 
approval and margins all constrain the pace of change, expect a two -stage adjustment: curtailment and demand 
destruction first, structural substitution and capex later , alongside rising demand for fuel/feedstock flexibility even 
where it costs more than a specialized, single-fuel plant.

Households to face higher inflation 
Six months in, households are losing purchasing power in stages.  Petrol and diesel reprice within days or weeks, 
but gas and electricity bills follow regulated tariff calendars and contract -renewal dates, so the current inflation 
reading is not yet the peak. Exposure varies sharply by household - an EV- and heat-pump-owning homeowner is 
comparatively insulated, a gas-heated renter won’t feel the full effect until tariffs reset, and a rural household reliant 
on diesel cars feels it immediately. Petrol and diesel are the fastest channel: European pu mp prices typically pass 
through crude costs within one to two months, roughly one -for-one. In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of 
August’s CPI increase, diesel peaked above 6  USD/gallon in September, and average pump prices are on track for 
3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y) - about USD 355 more per year for a typical household; hitting rural, low-income 
households hardest given limited access to public transport.  
Gas and electricity bills rise on institutional timetables, so the pass -through is felt with a lag that varies by 
market. In France, the regulated gas reference price rises 6.3% on October 1 (to 182.88  EUR/MWh including tax, 
about 60 EUR/year for indexed contracts), though roughly 40% of households are on fixed-price contracts and won’t 
feel it until renewal; French electricity moves more slowly, with the next adjustment scheduled for February 2027. 
The UK’s quarterly price cap rises by a bout 5  GBP/month from October, partly offset by government VAT relief, 
while roughly 35% of households on fixed contracts are shielded for now. Japan and India rely more on subsidy 
calendars than market pass -through, which smooths the household shock across time wit hout removing its fiscal 
cost. More broadly, real energy bills were still about 4% above 2019 levels in 2024, so this shock lands on budgets 
that had not fully recovered from 2022 ; and unevenly: an estimated 120mn households in advanced economies 
spend over 10% of income on home energy, rising to 20% including transport for the poorest, and the bottom income 
quintile spent 12% of disposable income on gas, electricity and heating in 2022 versus 4% for the top quintile.

In the longer term, the energy crisis impacts inflation beyond energy bills.  A second inflationary wave builds as 
transport and industrial costs feed into food, goods and services with a lag of around two years: a permanent 10% 
rise in oil prices raises headline CPI by about 2.3%, food CPI by 0.3% and core CPI by 0.1%, and after e xpenditure 
weights, headline CPI rises by almost 0.4% - consistent with the roughly 0.5% inflation contribution the last oil move 
added in the Eurozone, UK and Canada. Looking ahead, households are likely to weigh energy resilience more 
heavily in purchasing decisions, shifting spending from variable fuel cost s toward upfront capex on electrification 
and insulation - even as higher prices simultaneously squeeze the ability to finance that switch, widening inequality 
in energy exposure. Electricity is set to become the central household energy price, trading oil and gas exposure for 
exposure to power prices, network ch arges and time -of-use pricing, with government subsidies and price -cap 
calendars still deciding when the crisis is actually felt.

The energy-yield loop 
Energy shocks often have an immediate effect on rates through several components.  The first one is inflation 
expectations: Higher commodity prices tend to increase inflation and are priced directly on the derivatives market . 
Historically a 1% rise in Brent lifts the 2Y inflation swap by 1. 65bps in the US and 1. 77bps in the Eurozone, and the 
1y1y forward inflation swap by 0.64bp in the US and 1.09bp in the Eurozone. During special shocks the effect 
intensifies: The US 2Y inflation swap moved 1. 86bps per 1% of oil during the Ukraine shock and 2. 01bps during the 
Iran episode. The EU 2Y inflation swap moved by 2.60bps during the Ukraine shock, but only 0.36bp during the Iran 
episode. 
The first channel then impacts  the expected short rates path.  When inflation tends to increase suddenly, the 
central bank faces more pressure  to be hawkish because of market pricing . Historically a 1% rise in Brent adds 
1.02bp to US monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y OIS − Fed Funds) and 0.83bp to EUR monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y 
OIS − €STR). During crises , this pressure concentrates region by region: US pricing repriced hardest during the 
Ukraine shock at 2.82bps per 1% of oil, close to three times its long -run sensitivity, while in the current Iran episode

it is EUR pricing that dominates at 2.40bps. This second channel tends to move short rates (more sensitive to inflation 
and monetary policy) (<2Y maturities: US 2Y +0.95bp, DE 2Y +0.53bp per 1% of oil) and the "belly" (more sensitive 
to growth and the economy) (5Y –7Y: US 5Y +0.98bp, DE 5Y +0.54bp) fa ster than long rates (which embed more 
fundamental factors such as demography, fiscal situation, political stability) (>10Y maturity: US 30Y +0.65bp, DE 
30Y +0.50bp). When the front end increases more than the long end, it induces a bear flattening , which hurts the 
economy more broadly. This tends to tighten financial conditions by increasing borrowing costs, slowing the growth 
of production and credit, but also by increasing transport costs, making it harder for commodities trading houses to 
provide more inputs to markets due to their short -term financing at 3M.

In times of shocks, the sensitivities of commodities on rates roughly double . On the German curve, a 1% rise in 
Brent moves the 2Y yield by 0.53bp historically versus 1.43bp in the current episode, and EUR monetary pricing by 
0.83bp versus 2.40bp. Per +10% move in Brent, that is about +5bps on the German curve and +9bps on the US curve 
in normal times, rising to about +14bps on the German 2Y and +12bps on the US 10Y in shock, with the 2Y inflation 
swaps moving some + 17bps historically and up to + 20bps ina  shock.  Figure 9 illustrates the incremental impact 
on rates and inflation markets implied by a move in Brent to USD120, USD100 or USD80 from current levels. 
These scenario moves are separate from the repricing already observed during the current episode, much of 
which is already embedded in inflation compensation and monetary-policy expectations.

Bond markets have already absorbed a large portion of the current energy shock through higher inflation 
compensation and a repricing of the expected monetary -policy path. While additional oil-price gains would still 
exert upward pressure on rates, the scenario analysis suggests that the marginal impact is now smaller than during 
the initial phase of the shock. As a result, a sustained and significant further rise in yields would likely require a new 
escalation in supply disruptions or clearer evidence that energy -driven inflation is feeding into broader price 
dynamics and delaying the normalization of monetary policy.  
How the conflict is reshaping the global energy map 
The Strait of Hormuz has always been the Gulf’s structural vulnerability, and this crisis is accelerating a shift 
from managing chokepoint risk to actively engineering around it.  The attack on Saudi Arabia’s own hedge (i.e.  
Petroline) is a reminder that existing bypass infrastructure is now a target, not a guarantee. Riyadh’s repair timeline 
will determine how quickly that redundancy returns, but the episode has already revived talk of expanding capacity 
rather than just repairing it. The UAE’s ADCOP pipeline to Fujairah, which sits outside the Strait entirely, remains the 
cleanest working bypass and has absorbed a large share of redirected flows, with further capacity additions 
planned for 2027. Iraq is leaning on the Kirkuk -Ceyhan pipeline and the broader Development Road corridor to 
Türkiye, while Oman’s Duqm port is the other candidate for a Gulf pipeline that bypasses the Strait altogether.

There is no easy fix for natural gas.  Unlike crude, LNG cannot be pipelined around Hormuz, which is why Qatari 
and Emirati LNG loadings have collapsed even as oil exports partially recovered via redirection. With normalization 
pushed out to Q1 2027, Qatar’s pattern of sending more empty tanke rs into the Gulf is one of the few signs of an 
eventual ramp-up, but there is no Petroline-equivalent for gas and the exposure is structural. 
On the demand side, the pressure to de -risk predates this crisis — it dates back to the war in Ukraine — and is 
now intensifying, though Russia/Ukraine plays no direct role in the current Hormuz shock beyond the refined -
products channel already noted.  China has spent the past decade building overland alternatives (the ESPO 
pipeline from Russia and the Kazakhstan -China link) specifically to cut seaborne Gulf and Malacca dependency, 
and its crude imports remain about 4mbpd below 2025 levels even at firm prices — though the recent uptick in 
Chinese buying is a refining -margin and re -export story more than fresh demand. India is diversifying toward US, 
West African and discounted Russian barrels via the Cape route while expanding strategic reserves, and Japan and 
South Korea are rebuilding SPRs drawn down earlier in the crisis. The India -Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor 
is likely to see fresh political momentum as a longer-term, non-maritime link between the Gulf and Europe. None of

### Page 10

10

this displaces Hormuz as the region’s central artery near -term, but it marks a durable shift toward supply -side 
redundancy and demand-side diversification that will outlast the current conflict.

MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.
